In general, military exercises are held with foreign forces once or twice per week.[^cite_note-MindefApr2005-251] Due to airspace and land constraints, the [Republic of Singapore Air Force](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Republic_of_Singapore_Air_Force) (RSAF) maintains a number of overseas bases in [Australia](4689264.md), the United States, and [France](5843419.md). The RSAF's 130 Squadron is based in [RAAF Base Pearce](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/RAAF_Base_Pearce), [Western Australia](33613.md),[^cite_note-266] and its [126 Squadron](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/126_Squadron,_Republic_of_Singapore_Air_Force) is based in the [Oakey Army Aviation Centre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oakey_Army_Aviation_Centre), [Queensland](59051.md).[^cite_note-267] The RSAF has one squadron—the 150 Squadron—based in [Cazaux Air Base](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cazaux_Air_Base) in southern France.[^cite_note-268] The RSAF's overseas detachments in the United States are: [Luke Air Force Base](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luke_Air_Force_Base) in [Arizona](21883824.md), [Marana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marana,_Arizona) in Arizona, [Mountain Home Air Force Base](55328338.md) in [Idaho](14607.md), and [Andersen Air Force Base](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Andersen_Air_Force_Base) in [Guam](11974.md).[^cite_note-269][^cite_note-270][^cite_note-271]

The SAF has sent forces to assist in operations outside the country, in areas such as [Iraq](7515928.md),[^cite_note-272] and [Afghanistan](737.md),[^cite_note-273][^cite_note-274] in both military and civilian roles. In the region, they have helped to stabilise [East Timor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/East_Timor)[^cite_note-275] and have provided aid to [Aceh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aceh) in Indonesia following the [2004 Indian Ocean earthquake and tsunami](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2004_Indian_Ocean_earthquake_and_tsunami).[^cite_note-276] Since 2009, the [Republic of Singapore Navy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Republic_of_Singapore_Navy) (RSN) has deployed ships to the [Gulf of Aden](58716.md) to aid in countering [piracy](50715.md) efforts as part of [Task Force 151](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Combined_Task_Force_151).[^cite_note-277] The SAF also helped in relief efforts during [Hurricane Katrina](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hurricane_Katrina),[^cite_note-278] and [Typhoon Haiyan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Typhoon_Haiyan).[^cite_note-279] Singapore is part of the [Five Power Defence Arrangements](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Five_Power_Defence_Arrangements) (FPDA), a military alliance with Australia, Malaysia, New Zealand, and the United Kingdom.[^cite_note-MindefApr2005-251] According to the 2024 [Global Peace Index](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Global_Peace_Index), Singapore is the 5th most peaceful country in the world.[^cite_note-280]

### Human rights

Main article: [Human rights in Singapore](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_rights_in_Singapore)

See also: [Abortion in Singapore](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abortion_in_Singapore), [Capital punishment in Singapore](3278380.md), and [LGBT rights in Singapore](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/LGBT_rights_in_Singapore)

[Capital punishment](5902.md) is a legal and enforced penalty in Singapore. The country is [one of four](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Capital_punishment_by_country) in the [developed world](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Developed_country) to retain the death penalty, along with the [United States](412425.md), [Japan](4967816.md) and [Taiwan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Capital_punishment_in_Taiwan). Particularly, its [use against drug trafficking](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Capital_punishment_for_drug_trafficking) has been a source of contention with [non-governmental organisations](46539.md) such as [Amnesty International](18947898.md) and [Human Rights Watch](46530.md).[^cite_note-281][^cite_note-282] The government has responded that it has "no doubts" that it is the right policy and that there is "clear evidence" of [serious deterrence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deterrence_\(penology\)), and that the law should be looked at upon in the wider context of "saving lives", particularly citizens.[^cite_note-283] In 2004, Amnesty International claimed that some legal provisions of the Singapore system for the death penalty conflict with "the right to be [presumed innocent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Presumption_of_innocence) until proven guilty".[^cite_note-amnesty-284] The government has disputed Amnesty's claims, stating that their "position on abolition of the death penalty is by no means uncontested internationally" and that the report contains "grave errors of facts and misrepresentations".[^cite_note-response-285]

From 1938 to 2023, sexual relations between men were technically illegal under [Section 377A of the Penal Code](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Section_377A_of_the_Penal_Code_\(Singapore\)), first introduced during British colonial rule.[^cite_note-\:7-286] During the last few decades, this law was mostly unenforced and pressure to repeal it increased as homosexuality became more accepted by Singaporean society.[^cite_note-287] Meanwhile, sexual relations between women had always been legal.[^cite_note-\:7-286] In 2022, Prime Minister [Lee Hsien Loong](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lee_Hsien_Loong) announced that Singapore would repeal 377A, effectively decriminalising homosexual behaviour. Nevertheless, he added that the repeal will not affect the recognition of "traditional familial and societal norms," including how marriage is defined, leaving the legal status of [same-sex marriage](92656.md) unchanged for the time, although the possibility of [civil unions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Civil_unions) was not officially ruled out.[^cite_note-288] Lee described this as a compromise between the [conservative](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conservative) (and often religious) and [progressive](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Progressivism) elements of Singaporean society to prevent further political fracturing.[^cite_note-289] The law was officially repealed on 3 January 2023.[^cite_note-290]

[Pink Dot SG](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pink_Dot_SG), an event held in support of the [LGBT](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/LGBT) community, has drawn thousands of people annually since 2009 with increasing attendance.[^cite_note-291] According to a survey conducted by the [Institute of Policy Studies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Institute_of_Policy_Studies_\(Singapore\)) in 2019, Singaporean society has become more [liberal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liberalism) on LGBT rights. In the survey, more than 20% of people said that sexual relations between adults of the same sex were not wrong at all or not wrong most of the time, up from 10% in 2013. The survey found that 27% felt the same way about same-sex marriage (an increase from 15% in 2013) and 30% did so about same-sex couples adopting a child (an increase from 24% in 2013).[^cite_note-292][^cite_note-293]

[Pimps](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Procuring_\(prostitution\)) often [traffic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sex_trafficking_in_Singapore) women from neighbouring countries such as [China](5405.md), [Malaysia](3607937.md) and [Vietnam](202354.md) at their [brothels](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brothels) as well as rented [apartments](265567.md) and [hostels](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hostels) for higher profit margins when they get a cut from customers.[^cite_note-294][^cite_note-295] In response, amendments were made to the [Women's Charter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Women's_Charter) by the government in 2019 to legislate more serious punishments for traffickers, including imprisonment of up to seven years and a fine of S\$100,000.[^cite_note-296]

## Economy

Main article: [Economy of Singapore](57011.md)



Skyline of Singapore's [Downtown Core](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Downtown_Core)

Singapore has a highly developed [market economy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Market_economy), based historically on extended [entrepôt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Entrep%C3%B4t) trade. Along with [Hong Kong](13404.md), [South Korea](27019.md), and [Taiwan](25734.md), Singapore is one of the [Four Asian Tigers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Four_Asian_Tigers), and has surpassed its peers in terms of [Gross Domestic Product](12594.md) (GDP) per capita. Between 1965 and 1995, growth rates averaged around 6 per cent per annum, transforming the living standards of the population.[^cite_note-297]

The Singaporean economy is regarded as free, innovative,[^cite_note-298] dynamic[^cite_note-299] and business-friendly.[^cite_note-300] For several years, Singapore has been one of the few[^cite_note-301] countries with a [AAA credit rating](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bond_credit_rating) from the [big three](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Big_Three_\(credit_rating_agencies\)), and the only Asian country to achieve this rating.[^cite_note-302] Singapore attracts a large amount of foreign investment as a result of its location, skilled workforce, low tax rates, advanced infrastructure and zero-tolerance against corruption.[^cite_note-303] It was the world's 4th most competitive economy in 2023, according to the [International Institute for Management Development](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Institute_for_Management_Development)'s World Competitiveness Ranking of 64 countries,[^cite_note-304] with the [highest GDP (PPP) per capita](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_GDP_\(PPP\)_per_capita).[^cite_note-305][^cite_note-306][^cite_note-307] Roughly 44 per cent of the Singaporean workforce is made up of non-Singaporeans.[^cite_note-308] Despite market freedom, Singapore's government operations have a significant stake in the economy, contributing 22% of the GDP.[^cite_note-309] The city is a popular location for conferences and events.[^cite_note-310]

Singapore Airlines celebrated the nation's Golden Jubilee with a flag livery on its Airbus A380

[Singapore Airlines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Singapore_Airlines), the country's [flag carrier](465816.md), celebrated the nation's 2015 [Golden Jubilee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Golden_Jubilee) with a flag [livery](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Livery) on its [Airbus A380](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Airbus_A380).

The currency of Singapore is the [Singapore dollar](476174.md) (SGD or S\$), issued by the [Monetary Authority of Singapore](287797.md) (MAS).[^cite_note-311] It has been interchangeable with the [Brunei dollar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brunei_dollar) at [par value](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Par_value) since 1967.[^cite_note-Currency_History-312] MAS manages its [monetary policy](297032.md) by allowing the Singapore dollar [exchange rate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Exchange_rate) to rise or fall within an undisclosed trading band. This is different from most [central banks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Central_banks), which use [interest rates](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Interest_rates) to manage policy.[^cite_note-313] Singapore has the world's eleventh [largest foreign reserves](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_foreign_exchange_reserves),[^cite_note-314] and one of the highest [net international investment position per capita](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_creditor_nations_by_net_international_investment_position_per_capita).[^cite_note-315][^cite_note-316]

Singapore has been identified as a [tax haven](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tax_haven)[^cite_note-317] for the wealthy due to its low tax rates on personal income and tax exemptions on foreign-based income and capital gains. Individuals such as Australian millionaire retailer [Brett Blundy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brett_Blundy) and multi-billionaire Facebook co-founder [Eduardo Saverin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eduardo_Saverin) are two examples of wealthy individuals who have settled in Singapore.[^cite_note-318] In 2009, Singapore was removed from the [Organisation for Economic Co-operation and Development](33853117.md) (OECD) "liste grise" of tax havens,[^cite_note-319] and ranked fourth on the [Tax Justice Network](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tax_Justice_Network)'s 2015 [Financial Secrecy Index](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Financial_Secrecy_Index) of the world's off-shore financial service providers, banking one-eighth of the world's offshore capital, while "providing numerous tax avoidance and evasion opportunities".[^cite_note-FSI-320] In August 2016, *[The Straits Times](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Straits_Times)* reported that Indonesia had decided to create tax havens on two islands near Singapore to bring Indonesian capital back into the tax base.[^cite_note-321] In October 2016, the Monetary Authority of Singapore admonished and fined [UBS](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/UBS) and [DBS](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/DBS_Bank) and withdrew the banking licence from [Falcon Private Bank](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Falcon_Private_Bank?action=edit\&redlink=1) for their alleged role in the Malaysian Sovereign Fund scandal.[^cite_note-322][^cite_note-323]

In 2016, Singapore was rated the world's most expensive city for the third consecutive year by the [Economist Intelligence Unit](15303116.md),[^cite_note-324][^cite_note-325] and this remained true in 2018.[^cite_note-326] The government provides numerous assistance programmes to the homeless and needy through the [Ministry of Social and Family Development](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ministry_of_Social_and_Family_Development), so acute poverty is rare. Some of the programmes include providing financial assistance to needy households, providing free medical care at government hospitals, and paying for children's tuition.[^cite_note-327][^cite_note-328][^cite_note-329] Other benefits include compensation for gym fees to encourage citizens to exercise,[^cite_note-330] up to S\$166,000 as a [baby bonus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baby_bonus) for each citizen,[^cite_note-331] heavily subsidised healthcare, financial aid for the disabled, the provision of reduced-cost laptops for poor students,[^cite_note-332] rebates for costs such as public transport[^cite_note-333] and utility bills, and more.[^cite_note-334][^cite_note-335] In the 2025 report, Singapore was ranked 13th in the world in the [Human Development Index](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_Development_Index) (HDI) with a value of 0.946, making it one of four regions in Asia to be ranked within the top 20, with the other three being Hong Kong, United Arab Emirates, and South Korea.[^cite_note-HDI-13]

### Transport

Main article: [Transport in Singapore](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transport_in_Singapore)

#### Land



A [R151](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alstom_Movia_R151) MRT train leaving [Eunos station](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eunos_station)



[Public bus](1159446.md) in Singapore

Singapore's public transport network is shaped up with trains (consisting of the [MRT](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mass_Rapid_Transit_\(Singapore\)) and [LRT](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Light_Rail_Transit_\(Singapore\)) systems), [buses](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bus_transport_in_Singapore) and [taxis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taxis_of_Singapore). There are currently six MRT lines ([North–South Line](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North%E2%80%93South_Line_\(Singapore\)), [East–West Line](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/East%E2%80%93West_Line_\(Singapore\)), [North East Line](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North_East_Line), [Circle Line](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Circle_Line_\(Singapore\)), [Downtown Line](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Downtown_Line) and [Thomson–East Coast Line](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thomson%E2%80%93East_Coast_Line)), three LRT lines serving the neighbourhoods of [Bukit Panjang](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bukit_Panjang) and [Choa Chu Kang](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Choa_Chu_Kang) ([Bukit Panjang LRT line](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bukit_Panjang_LRT_line)), [Sengkang](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sengkang) ([Sengkang LRT line](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sengkang_LRT_line)) and [Punggol](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Punggol) ([Punggol LRT line](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Punggol_LRT_line)),[^cite_note-336] covering around 241 km (150 mi) in total, and more than 300 bus routes in operation.[^cite_note-337] [Taxis](22026933.md) are a popular form of transport as the fares are relatively affordable when compared to many other developed countries, whilst cars in Singapore are the most expensive to own worldwide.[^cite_note-338]

Singapore has a [road system](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Road_transport_in_Singapore) covering 3,356 kilometres (2,085 mi), which includes 161 kilometres (100 mi) [of expressways](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Expressways_of_Singapore).[^cite_note-339][^cite_note-340] The [Singapore Area Licensing Scheme](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Singapore_Area_Licensing_Scheme), implemented in 1975, became the world's first [congestion pricing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Congestion_pricing) scheme, and included other complementary measures such as stringent car ownership quotas and improvements in mass transit.[^cite_note-341][^cite_note-342] Upgraded in 1998 and renamed [Electronic Road Pricing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Electronic_Road_Pricing) (ERP), the system introduced [electronic toll collection](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Electronic_toll_collection), electronic detection, and video surveillance technology.[^cite_note-343] A satellite-based system was due to replace the physical gantries by 2020, but has been delayed until 2026 due to global shortages in the supply of semiconductors.[^cite_note-344] As Singapore is a small island with a high population density, the number of private cars on the road is restricted with a pre-set car population quota, to curb pollution and congestion. Car buyers must pay for Additional Registration Fees (ARF) duties of either 100%, 140%, 180% or 220% of the vehicle's Open Market Value (OMV), and bid for a Singaporean [Certificate of Entitlement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Certificate_of_Entitlement) (COE) (that varies twice a month in supply based on the number of car registrations and de-registrations), which allows the car to be driven on the road for maximum period of 10 years. Car prices are generally significantly higher in Singapore than in other English-speaking countries.[^cite_note-BloombergBMW-345] As with most Commonwealth countries, vehicles on the road and people walking on the streets keep to the left ([left-hand traffic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Left-_and_right-hand_traffic)).[^cite_note-346]

In 2025, Singapore started actively engaging in autonomous vehicle testing. In November 2025, the [Land Transport Authority](434512.md) (LTA) approved [WeRide](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/WeRide) and [Grab](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grab_Holdings) to test 11 autonomous vehicles on two [Punggol](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Punggol) shuttle routes after initial tests in October, and aim for public passengers by early 2026.[^cite_note-347]

The [Johor–Singapore Causeway](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Johor%E2%80%93Singapore_Causeway) (connecting Singapore with [Johor Bahru](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Johor_Bahru), Malaysia) is the [busiest international land border crossing](288329.md#Land) in the world, whereby approximately 350,000 travellers cross the border checkpoints of both [Woodlands Checkpoint](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Woodlands_Checkpoint) and [Sultan Iskandar Building](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sultan_Iskandar_Building) daily (with an annual total of 128 million travellers).[^cite_note-348] The [Land Transport Authority](434512.md) (LTA) is responsible for all land transport-related infrastructure and operations in Singapore.

#### Air



The Rain Vortex at [Jewel Changi Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jewel_Changi_Airport)

Singapore is a major international [transport hub](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transport_hub) in [Asia](689.md), serving some of the busiest sea and air trade routes. [Changi Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Changi_Airport) is an aviation centre for [Southeast Asia](28741.md) and a stopover on [Qantas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Qantas)' [Kangaroo Route](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kangaroo_Route) between [Sydney](266313.md) and [London](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Heathrow_Airport).[^cite_note-349] There are two civilian airports in Singapore, [Changi Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Singapore_Changi_Airport) and [Seletar Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seletar_Airport).[^cite_note-350][^cite_note-351] Changi Airport hosts a network of over 100 airlines connecting Singapore to some 300 cities in about 70 countries and territories worldwide.[^cite_note-352] It has been rated one of the best international airports by international travel magazines, including being rated as the world's best airport for the first time in 2006 by [Skytrax](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Skytrax).[^cite_note-353] It also had three of the ten [busiest international air routes in the world](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_busiest_passenger_flight_routes) in 2023: the busiest between [Kuala Lumpur](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kuala_Lumpur_International_Airport)–Singapore, the seventh busiest between [Jakarta](1203751.md)–Singapore, and the ninth busiest between [Bangkok](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Suvarnabhumi_Airport)–Singapore.[^cite_note-354]

[Singapore Airlines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Singapore_Airlines), the [flag carrier](465816.md) of Singapore,[^cite_note-355] has been regarded as a 5-star airline by Skytrax[^cite_note-356] and been in the world top 10 list of airlines for multiple consecutive years.[^cite_note-357] It held the title of the World's Best Airline by Skytrax in 2023. It won this title 12 times. Its hub, [Changi Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Changi_Airport) had also been rated as the world's best airport from 2013 to 2020 before being superseded by [Hamad International Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hamad_International_Airport) in [Doha](26214389.md).[^cite_note-358] It reclaimed this title in 2023[^cite_note-359] before being superseded once more in 2024.[^cite_note-360]

#### Sea



Port of Singapore viewed from The Pinnacle (2015)

The [Port of Singapore](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Port_of_Singapore), managed by port operators [PSA International](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/PSA_International) and [Jurong Port](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jurong_Port), was the world's second-busiest port in 2019 in terms of shipping tonnage handled, at 2.85 billion [gross tons](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gross_ton) (GT), and in terms of [containerised](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Container_\(cargo\)) traffic, at 37.2 million [twenty-foot equivalent units](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Twenty-foot_equivalent_unit) (TEUs).[^cite_note-MPA-361] It is also the world's second-busiest, behind Shanghai, in terms of cargo tonnage with 626 million tons handled. In addition, the port is the world's busiest for [transshipment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transshipment) traffic and the world's biggest ship refuelling centre.[^cite_note-BTP-362]

### Industry sectors

Singapore is the world's third-largest [foreign exchange](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Foreign_exchange_market) centre, sixth-largest [financial centre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Financial_centre),[^cite_note-GFCI-363] second-largest casino gambling market,[^cite_note-edmontonjournal.com-364] third-largest oil-refining and trading centre, largest oil-rig producer and hub for ship repair services,[^cite_note-365][^cite_note-366][^cite_note-367] and largest logistics hub.[^cite_note-368] The economy is diversified, with its top contributors being financial services, manufacturing, and oil-refining. Its main exports are refined petroleum, integrated circuits, and computers,[^cite_note-369] which constituted 27% of the country's GDP in 2010. Other significant sectors include electronics, chemicals, mechanical engineering, and biomedical sciences. Singapore was ranked 5th in the [Global Innovation Index](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Global_Innovation_Index) in 2025.[^cite_note-370][^cite_note-371] In 2019, there were more than 60 semiconductor companies in Singapore, which together constituted 11% of the global market share. The semiconductor industry alone contributes around 7% of Singapore's GDP.[^cite_note-372]

Singapore's largest companies are in the [telecommunications](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Telecommunications_in_Singapore), [banking](57011.md#Banking), [transportation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transport_in_Singapore), and manufacturing sectors, many of which started as state-run [statutory corporations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Statutory_corporation) and have since been publicly listed on the [Singapore Exchange](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Singapore_Exchange). Such companies include [Singapore Telecommunications](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Singapore_Telecommunications) (Singtel), [Singapore Technologies Engineering](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Singapore_Technologies_Engineering), [Keppel Corporation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Keppel_Corporation), [Oversea-Chinese Banking Corporation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oversea-Chinese_Banking_Corporation) (OCBC), [Development Bank of Singapore](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Development_Bank_of_Singapore) (DBS), and [United Overseas Bank](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Overseas_Bank) (UOB). In 2011, after the [2008 financial crisis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2008_financial_crisis), OCBC, DBS and UOB were ranked by *[Bloomberg Businessweek](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bloomberg_Businessweek)* as the world's first, fifth, and sixth strongest banks in the world, respectively.[^cite_note-373] It is home to the headquarters of three [*Fortune* Global 500](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fortune_Global_500) companies, the highest in the region.[^cite_note-374]

The nation's best known global companies include [Singapore Airlines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Singapore_Airlines), [Changi Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Changi_Airport), and the [Port of Singapore](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Port_of_Singapore), all of which are among the most-awarded in their respective fields. Singapore Airlines was ranked as Asia's most-admired company, and the world's 19th most-admired company in 2015 by *[Fortune](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fortune_\(magazine\))*'s annual "50 most admired companies in the world" industry surveys. Other awards it has received include the US-based *[Travel + Leisure](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Travel_+_Leisure)*'s Best International Airline award, which it has won for 20 consecutive years.[^cite_note-375][^cite_note-376] Changi Airport connects over 100 airlines to more than 300 cities. The strategic international air hub has more than 480 World's Best Airport awards as of 2015, and is known as the most-awarded airport in the world.[^cite_note-377] Over ten [free-trade agreements](2533562.md) have been signed with other countries and regions.[^cite_note-govt.nz-230] Singapore is the second-largest foreign investor in India.[^cite_note-378] It is the 14th largest exporter and the 15th largest importer in the world.[^cite_note-379][^cite_note-380]

#### Tourism

Main article: [Tourism in Singapore](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tourism_in_Singapore)



The [Merlion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merlion), the official mascot of Singapore

Tourism is a major industry and contributor to the [Singaporean economy](57011.md), attracting 13.6 million international tourists in 2023, more than double Singapore's total population.[^cite_note-381] Tourism contributed directly to about 3% of Singapore's GDP, on average, in the 10 years before 2023, excluding the [COVID-19 pandemic](62750956.md) years.[^cite_note-382] Altogether, the sector generated approximately 8.6% of Singapore's employment in 2016.[^cite_note-WTTCreport2017-383]

Well-known landmarks include the [Merlion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merlion),[^cite_note-384] the [Esplanade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Esplanade_%E2%80%93_Theatres_on_the_Bay),[^cite_note-\:2-385] [Marina Bay Sands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marina_Bay_Sands),[^cite_note-386] [Gardens by the Bay](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gardens_by_the_Bay),[^cite_note-387] [Jewel Changi Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jewel_Changi_Airport),[^cite_note-388] [CHIJMES](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/CHIJMES),[^cite_note-\:2-385] [National Gallery Singapore](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Gallery_Singapore),[^cite_note-\:2-385] the [Singapore Flyer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Singapore_Flyer),[^cite_note-\:2-385] the [Orchard Road](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orchard_Road) shopping belt,[^cite_note-Orchard-389] the resort island of [Sentosa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sentosa),[^cite_note-390] and the [Singapore Botanic Gardens](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Singapore_Botanic_Gardens), Singapore's first [UNESCO World Heritage Site](44940.md),[^cite_note-391] all located in southern and eastern Singapore.



[Raffles Hotel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Raffles_Hotel)&#x20;

The [Singapore Tourism Board](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Singapore_Tourism_Board) (STB) is the [statutory board](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Statutory_boards_of_the_Singapore_Government) under the [Ministry of Trade and Industry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ministry_of_Trade_and_Industry_\(Singapore\)) which is tasked with the promotion of the country's tourism industry. In August 2017 the STB and the Economic Development Board (EDB) unveiled a unified brand, Singapore – [Passion Made Possible](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Passion_Made_Possible), to market Singapore internationally for tourism and business purposes.[^cite_note-392] The [Orchard Road](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orchard_Road) district, which contains multi-storey shopping centres and hotels, can be considered the centre of shopping and tourism in Singapore.[^cite_note-Orchard-389] Other popular tourist attractions include the [Singapore Zoo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Singapore_Zoo), [River Wonders](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/River_Wonders), [Bird Paradise](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bird_Paradise) and [Night Safari](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Night_Safari,_Singapore) (located in Northern Singapore). The Singapore Zoo has embraced the open zoo concept whereby animals are kept in enclosures, separated from visitors by hidden dry or wet moats, instead of caging the animals, and the River Wonders has 300 species of animals, including numerous endangered species.[^cite_note-393] Singapore promotes itself as a [medical tourism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Medical_tourism) hub, with about 200,000 foreigners seeking medical care there each year. Singapore medical services aim to serve at least one million foreign patients annually and generate US\$3 billion in revenue.[^cite_note-394]

In 2025, Singapore saw a record number of nearly 245 million people crossing its borders. In the same year, Singapore turned away more than 45,000 foreigners – 38% higher than 2024.[^cite_note-395]

Luxury Hotels in Singapore include [Raffles Hotel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Raffles_Hotel), [Marina Bay Sands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marina_Bay_Sands), [Grand Hyatt Singapore](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grand_Hyatt_Singapore), [Capella Singapore](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Capella_Singapore), [Conrad Singapore](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conrad_Singapore_Marina_Bay), [Hilton Singapore Orchard](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hilton_Singapore_Orchard), [Shangri-La Singapore](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shangri-La_Hotel,_Singapore) and [The Ritz-Carlton Millenia Singapore](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Ritz-Carlton_Millenia_Singapore).

## Demographics

Main articles: [Demographics of Singapore](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Demographics_of_Singapore) and [Singaporeans](38208458.md)

See also: [Race in Singapore](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Race_in_Singapore)



Chinese ([East Asian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/East_Asian_people)), Malay ([Southeast Asian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indigenous_peoples_of_Southeast_Asia)), and Indian ([South Asian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Asian_ethnic_groups)) women in Singapore, c. 1890. To promote racial harmony among the three races, a unique [Racial Harmony Day](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Racial_Harmony_Day) is celebrated on 21 July every year.

As of mid-2025, Singapore's total population stood at 6,110,200, of whom 3,660,200 (59.9%) were [citizens](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Singaporean_nationality_law) and the remaining 2,450,000 (40.1%) were either [permanent residents](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Permanent_residency_in_Singapore) (543,800, 8.9%) or [international students](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_student), [foreign workers](384142.md), or [dependants](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dependant) (1,906,700, 31.2%).[^cite_note-Population_in_Brief_2025-9] It is the [world's largest and most populous city-state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/City-state).[^cite_note-City-state-1][^cite_note-Population_in_Brief_2025-9]

The 2020 census reported that about 74.3% of residents were of Chinese descent, 13.5% of Malay descent, 9.0% of Indian descent, and 3.2% of other descent (such as [Eurasian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eurasians_in_Singapore)); this proportion was virtually identical to the 2010 census, with slight increases among Chinese and Malay (0.2% and 0.1% respectively) and minor decreases in Indian and others (0.2% and 0.1%).[^cite_note-396][^cite_note-2010census-397] Prior to 2010, each person could register as a member of only one race, by default that of their father; therefore, mixed-race persons were solely grouped under their father's race in government censuses. From 2010 onward, people may register using a multi-racial classification, in which they may choose one primary race and one secondary race, but no more than two.[^cite_note-398]

Like other developed countries in Asia, Singapore experienced a rapid decline in its [total fertility rate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Total_fertility_rate) (TFR) beginning in the 1980s.[^cite_note-imf.org-399] Since 2010, its TFR has largely plateaued at 1.1 children per woman, which is among the lowest in the world and well below the 2.1 needed to replace the population.[^cite_note-400] Consequently, the median age of Singaporean residents is among the highest in the world, at 42.8 in 2022 compared to 39.6 ten years earlier.[^cite_note-401] Starting in 2001, the government [introduced a series of programmes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Population_planning_in_Singapore) to increase fertility, including paid maternity leave, childcare subsidies, tax relief and rebates, one-time cash gifts, and grants for companies that implement flexible work arrangements;[^cite_note-imf.org-399] nevertheless, live births have continued to decline, hitting a record low in 2022.[^cite_note-402] Singapore's immigration policy is designed to alleviate the decline and maintain its working-age population.[^cite_note-403][^cite_note-404][^cite_note-405]

91% of resident households (i.e. households headed by a Singapore citizen or permanent resident) own the homes they live in, and the average household size is 3.43 persons (which include dependants who are neither citizens nor permanent residents).[^cite_note-406][^cite_note-singstat2014h\&h-407] However, due to scarcity of land, 78.7% of resident households live in [subsidised, high-rise, public housing apartments](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Public_housing_in_Singapore) developed by the [Housing and Development Board](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Housing_and_Development_Board) (HDB). Also, 75.9% of resident households live in properties that are equal to, or larger than, a four-room (i.e. three bedrooms plus one living room) HDB flat or in private housing.[^cite_note-408][^cite_note-hdb-409] Live-in foreign [domestic workers](636781.md) are quite common in Singapore, with about 224,500 foreign domestic workers there, as of December 2013.[^cite_note-410]

### Languages

Main article: [Languages of Singapore](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Languages_of_Singapore)

Singapore has four official languages: [English](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Singaporean_English), [Mandarin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Singaporean_Mandarin), [Malay](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malay_language), and [Tamil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tamil_language).[^cite_note-statutes.agc.gov.sg-411]

| Language |  | Per cent |
| --- | --- | --- |
| English |  | 58.1% |
| Mandarin |  | 26.6% |
| Malay |  | 7.0% |
| Other Sinitic languages |  | 4.9% |
| Tamil |  | 2.2% |
| Others |  | 1.3% |

English is the [lingua franca](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lingua_franca)[^cite_note-Gupta_244–263-412][^cite_note-Dixon,_L._Quentin_2005_p._625-635-413][^cite_note-autogenerated1-414][^cite_note-TanShermanp340-341-415] and the main language used in business, government, law and education.[^cite_note-Britishcouncil-416][^cite_note-417] The [Constitution of Singapore](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitution_of_Singapore) and all government legislation is written in English, and [interpreters](899930.md) are required if a language other than English is used in the [Singaporean courts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Judicial_system_of_Singapore).[^cite_note-418][^cite_note-\:6-207] [Statutory corporations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Statutory_corporation) conduct their businesses in English, while any official documents written in a non-English official language such as Malay, Mandarin, or Tamil are typically translated into English to be accepted for use.[^cite_note-419][^cite_note-Dixon,_L._Quentin_2005_p._625-635-413][^cite_note-420]

[Malay](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malay_language) was designated as a national language by the Singaporean government after independence from Britain in the 1960s to avoid friction with Singapore's Malay-speaking neighbours of Malaysia and Indonesia.[^cite_note-thirdworldtofirst-217] It has a symbolic, rather than functional purpose.[^cite_note-statutes.agc.gov.sg-411][^cite_note-421][^cite_note-422] It is used in the national anthem *[Majulah Singapura](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Majulah_Singapura)*,[^cite_note-423] in citations of [Singaporean orders and decorations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Singaporean_orders_and_decorations) and in military commands.[^cite_note-424][^cite_note-singstat1-425] Singaporean Malay is officially written in the Latin-based [Rumi script](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rumi_script), though some Singaporean Malays also learn the Arabic-based [Jawi script](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jawi_alphabet).[^cite_note-426] Jawi is considered an ethnic script for use on Singaporean identity cards.[^cite_note-427]

Singaporeans are mostly [bilingual](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bilingual), typically with English as their common language and their [mother-tongue](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mother-tongue) as a second language taught in schools, in order to preserve each individual's ethnic identity and values. According to the 2025 census, English was the language most spoken at home, used by 58.1% of the population; Mandarin was next, spoken at home by 26.6%.[^cite_note-2025survey-428][^cite_note-BBC-429] Nearly half a million speak other ancestral Southern [varieties of Chinese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Varieties_of_Chinese), mainly [Hokkien](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hokkien), [Teochew](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Teochew_dialect), and [Cantonese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cantonese), as their home language, although the use of these is declining in favour of Mandarin or just English.[^cite_note-singstat.gov.sg-430] [Singapore Chinese characters](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Singapore_Chinese_characters) are [simplified characters](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Simplified_Chinese_characters).[^cite_note-431]

[Singaporean English](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Singapore_English) is largely based on [British English](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_English), owing to the country's status as a former [crown colony](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Colony_of_Singapore).[^cite_note-432] However, forms of English spoken in Singapore range from [Standard Singapore English](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Singapore_Standard_English) to a colloquial form known as [Singlish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Singlish), which is discouraged by the government as it claims it to be a "bad language" that handicaps Singaporeans, presenting an obstacle to learning standard English and rendering the speaker incomprehensible to everyone except to another Singlish speaker.[^cite_note-433] Standard Singapore English is fully understandable to all [Standard English](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Standard_English) speakers, while most English-speaking people do not understand Singlish. Nevertheless, Singaporeans have a strong sense of identity and connection to Singlish, whereby the existence of Singlish is recognised as a distinctive cultural marker for many Singaporeans.[^cite_note-\:3-434] As such, in recent times, the government has tolerated the [diglossia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Diglossia) of both Singlish and Standard English (only for those who are fluent in both), whilst continuously reinforcing the importance of Standard English amongst those who speak only Singlish (which is not [mutually intelligible](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mutual_intelligibility) with the Standard English of other [English-speaking countries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/English-speaking_country)).[^cite_note-\:3-434]

### Religion

Main article: [Religion in Singapore](922794.md)

Religion in Singapore (2025 census)

[^cite_note-census2025-6]

1. [Buddhism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buddhism_in_Singapore) (30.9%)

2. [No religion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Irreligion_in_Singapore) (23.9%)

3. [Christianity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christianity_in_Singapore) (17.1%)

4. [Islam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islam_in_Singapore) (15.0%)

5. [Taoism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taoism_in_Singapore) (7.30%)

6. [Hinduism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hinduism_in_Singapore) (5.40%)

7. Other religions (0.40%)

[Buddhism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buddhism_in_Singapore) is the most widely practised religion, with 30.9% of residents declaring themselves adherents in the 2020 census. [Christianity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christianity_in_Singapore) was the second largest religion at 17.1%, followed by [Islam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islam_in_Singapore) (15.0%), [Taoism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taoism_in_Singapore) and [folk beliefs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chinese_folk_religion) (7.3%) and [Hinduism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hinduism_in_Singapore) (5.4%). Over one-fifth of the population had no religious affiliation.[^cite_note-census2025-6] The proportion of Christians, Muslims, and the nonreligious slightly increased between 2010 and 2020, while the proportion of Buddhists and Taoists slightly decreased; Hinduism and other faiths remained largely stable in their share of the population.[^cite_note-435][^cite_note-2010censuspercentage-436][^cite_note-437]

Singapore hosts monasteries and [Dharma](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dharma) centres from all three major traditions of Buddhism: [Theravada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Theravada), [Mahayana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mahayana), and [Vajrayana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vajrayana). Most Buddhists in Singapore are Chinese and adhere to the Mahayana tradition,[^cite_note-438] owing to decades of missionary activity from China. However, [Thailand's Theravada Buddhism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buddhism_in_Thailand) has seen growing popularity among the populace (not only the Chinese) during the past decade. [Soka Gakkai International](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soka_Gakkai_International), a Japanese Buddhist organisation, is practised by many people in Singapore, and mostly by those of Chinese descent. [Tibetan Buddhism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tibetan_Buddhism) has also made slow inroads into the country in recent years.[^cite_note-439]

### Education

Main article: [Education in Singapore](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Education_in_Singapore)

See also: [List of primary schools in Singapore](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_primary_schools_in_Singapore), [List of secondary schools in Singapore](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_secondary_schools_in_Singapore), and [List of universities in Singapore](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_universities_in_Singapore)



[Singapore University of Technology and Design](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Singapore_University_of_Technology_and_Design) (SUTD) campus

Education for primary, secondary, and tertiary levels is mostly supported by the state. All institutions, public and private, must be registered with the [Ministry of Education](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ministry_of_Education_\(Singapore\)) (MOE).[^cite_note-440] English is the language of instruction in all public schools,[^cite_note-441] and all subjects are taught and examined in English except for the "[mother tongue](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mother_tongue)" language paper.[^cite_note-442] While the term "mother tongue" in general refers to the first language internationally, in Singapore's education system, it is used to refer to the second language, as English is the first language.[^cite_note-443][^cite_note-444] Students who have been abroad for a while, or who struggle with their "Mother Tongue" language, are allowed to take a simpler syllabus or drop the subject.[^cite_note-445][^cite_note-446]

Education takes place in three stages: primary, secondary, and pre-university education, with the primary education being compulsory. Students begin with six years of primary school, which is made up of a four-year foundation course and a two-year orientation stage. The curriculum is focused on the development of English, the mother tongue, [mathematics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mathematics), and [science](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Natural_science).[^cite_note-Primary-447][^cite_note-448] Secondary school lasts from four to five years, and is divided between Express, Normal (Academic), and Normal (Technical) streams in each school, depending on a student's ability level.[^cite_note-Secondary-449] The basic coursework breakdown is the same as in the primary level, although classes are much more specialised.[^cite_note-450] Pre-university education takes place at either [Junior Colleges](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Junior_college_\(Singapore\)) (JCs) or the [Millennia Institute](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Millennia_Institute) (MI), over a period of two and three years respectively.[^cite_note-Pre-university-451] As alternatives to pre-university education, however, courses are offered in other post-secondary education institutions, including at [polytechnics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Institute_of_technology#Singapore) and [Institute of Technical Education](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Institute_of_Technical_Education) (ITE) colleges. Singapore has six public universities,[^cite_note-452] of which the [National University of Singapore](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_University_of_Singapore) (NUS) and [Nanyang Technological University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nanyang_Technological_University) (NTU) are among the top 20 universities in the world.[^cite_note-453]

National examinations are standardised across all schools, with a test taken after each stage. After the first six years of education, students take the [Primary School Leaving Examination](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Primary_School_Leaving_Examination) (PSLE),[^cite_note-Primary-447] which determines their placement at secondary school. At the end of the secondary stage, [GCE O-Level](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Singapore-Cambridge_GCE_Ordinary_Level) or [GCE N-Level](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Singapore-Cambridge_GCE_Normal_Level) exams are taken;[^cite_note-454] at the end of the following pre-university stage, the [GCE A-Level](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Singapore-Cambridge_GCE_Advanced_Level) exams are taken.[^cite_note-455] Some schools have a degree of freedom in their curriculum and are known as autonomous schools, for [secondary education](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Secondary_education_in_Singapore) level and above.[^cite_note-Secondary-449] Singapore is also an education hub, with more than 80,000 international students in 2006.[^cite_note-456] 5,000 Malaysian students cross the [Johor–Singapore Causeway](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Johor%E2%80%93Singapore_Causeway) daily to attend schools in Singapore.[^cite_note-457] In 2009, 20% of all students in Singaporean universities were international students—the maximum cap allowed, a majority from [ASEAN](106539.md), China and India.[^cite_note-458]

Singapore students have excelled in many of the world education benchmarks in maths, science and reading. In 2015, both its primary and secondary students rank first in [OECD](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Organisation_for_Economic_Co-operation_and_Development)'s global school performance rankings across 76 countries—described as the most comprehensive map of education standards.[^cite_note-459][^cite_note-460] In 2016, Singapore students topped both the [Programme for International Student Assessment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Programme_for_International_Student_Assessment) (PISA)[^cite_note-461][^cite_note-462][^cite_note-463][^cite_note-464] and the [Trends in International Mathematics and Science Study](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trends_in_International_Mathematics_and_Science_Study) (TIMSS).[^cite_note-465][^cite_note-466][^cite_note-467] In the 2016 EF English Proficiency Index taken in 72 countries, Singapore placed 6th and has been the only Asian country in the top 10.[^cite_note-468][^cite_note-469][^cite_note-470][^cite_note-471]

### Health

Main article: [Health in Singapore](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Health_in_Singapore)

See also: [Healthcare in Singapore](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Healthcare_in_Singapore)



[National University Hospital](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_University_Hospital) is the second largest hospital in the city, serving one million patients yearly.

Singapore has a generally efficient healthcare system, having achieved high quality of care while also keeping expenditures low.[^cite_note-Wyatt-472] The [World Health Organisation](33583.md) ranks Singapore's healthcare system as 6th overall in the world in its [World Health Report](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_Health_Report).[^cite_note-WHO-473] Singapore has had the [lowest infant mortality rates in the world](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_infant_mortality_rate) for the past two decades.[^cite_note-singstat2014births\&deaths-474] In 2019, Singaporeans had the longest life expectancy of any country at 84.8 years. Women can expect to live an average of 87.6 years with 75.8 years in good health. The averages are lower for men.[^cite_note-475] Singapore is ranked 1st on the [Global Food Security Index](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Global_Food_Security_Index).[^cite_note-476]

As of December 2011 and January 2013, 8,800 foreigners and 5,400 Singaporeans were respectively diagnosed with HIV,[^cite_note-477] but there are fewer than 10 annual deaths from HIV per 100,000 people. Adult obesity is below 10%.[^cite_note-autogenerated2-478] There is a high level of [immunisation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Immunisation).[^cite_note-479] In 2013, the [Economist Intelligence Unit](15303116.md) ranked Singapore as having the best quality of life in Asia and sixth overall in the world.[^cite_note-480]

The government's healthcare system is based upon the "3M" framework. This has three components: Medifund, which provides a safety net for those not able to otherwise afford healthcare; [Medisave](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Medisave), a compulsory national [medical savings account](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Medical_savings_account) system covering about 85% of the population; and Medishield, a government-funded health insurance programme. Public hospitals in Singapore have considerable autonomy in their management decisions, and notionally compete for patients, but remain in government ownership.[^cite_note-481] A subsidy scheme exists for those on low income.[^cite_note-482] In 2008, 32% of healthcare was funded by the government. Healthcare accounts for approximately 3.5% of Singapore's GDP.[^cite_note-483]

## Culture

Main article: [Culture of Singapore](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Culture_of_Singapore)



Ornate details on top of [Sri Mariamman Temple](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sri_Mariamman_Temple,_Singapore) in [Chinatown](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chinatown,_Singapore) district, Singapore's oldest Hindu temple since 1827

Despite its small size, Singapore has a diversity of languages, religions, and cultures.[^cite_note-singapore21-484] Former prime ministers Lee Kuan Yew and Goh Chok Tong stated that Singapore does not fit the traditional description of a nation, calling it a society-in-transition, pointing out the fact that Singaporeans do not all speak the same language, share the same religion, or have the same customs.[^cite_note-singapore21-484][^cite_note-485] Singaporeans who speak English as their native language would likely lean toward [Western culture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Western_culture) (along with either [Christian culture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christian_culture) or [secularism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Secularism)),[^cite_note-486] while those who speak Chinese as their native language mostly lean toward [Chinese culture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chinese_culture), which has linkages with [Chinese folk religion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chinese_folk_religion), [Buddhism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buddhism_in_Singapore), [Taoism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taoism_in_Singapore) and [Confucianism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confucianism). Malay-speaking Singaporeans mostly lean toward [Malay culture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malays_\(ethnic_group\)#Culture), which itself is closely linked to [Islamic culture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_culture).[^cite_note-487][^cite_note-\:8-488] Tamil-speaking Singaporeans mostly lean toward [Tamil culture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tamil_culture), which itself is mostly linked to [Hindu culture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hinduism). Racial and religious harmony is regarded as a crucial part of Singapore's success, and played a part in building a Singaporean identity.[^cite_note-489][^cite_note-\:8-488]

When Singapore became independent from the United Kingdom in 1963, most Singaporean citizens were transient [migrant labourers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Migrant_worker) who had no intention of staying permanently.[^cite_note-\:0-490] There was also a sizeable minority of middle-class, locally born people—known as [Peranakans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peranakan) or Baba-Nyonya-descendants of 15th- and 16th-century Chinese immigrants. With the exception of the Peranakans who pledged their loyalties to Singapore, most of the labourers' loyalties lay with their respective homelands of [Malaya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_Malaya), China and India. After independence, the government began a deliberate process of crafting a uniquely Singaporean identity and culture.[^cite_note-\:0-490] Singapore has a reputation as a [nanny state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nanny_state).[^cite_note-491][^cite_note-492] The government also places a heavy emphasis on [meritocracy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Meritocracy), where one is judged based on one's ability.[^cite_note-493]

The national flower of Singapore is the [hybrid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hybrid_plant) [orchid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orchid), [*Vanda* Miss Joaquim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Papilionanthe_Miss_Joaquim), named in memory of [Agnes Joaquim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Agnes_Joaquim), who crossbred the flower in her garden at [Tanjong Pagar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tanjong_Pagar) in 1893.[^cite_note-494] Singapore is known as the *Lion City* and many national symbols such as the [coat of arms](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coat_of_arms_of_Singapore) and the [lion head symbol](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lion_head_symbol_of_Singapore) make use of a lion. Major religious festivals are [public holidays](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Public_holidays_in_Singapore).[^cite_note-495] UNESCO recognises Singapore as a "[Design City](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Design_Cities_\(UNESCO\))."

### Arts

Further information: [Dance in Singapore](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dance_in_Singapore), [Singaporean literature](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Singaporean_literature), and [Music of Singapore](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Music_of_Singapore)

The National Gallery Singapore oversees the world's largest public collection of Southeast Asian and Singapore art

The [National Gallery Singapore](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Gallery_Singapore) oversees the world's largest public collection of Singaporean and Southeast Asian art.

During the 1990s the [National Arts Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Arts_Council_\(Singapore\)) was created to spearhead the development of performing arts, along with visual and literary art forms.[^cite_note-496] The [National Gallery Singapore](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Gallery_Singapore) is the nation's flagship museum with some 8,000 works from Singaporean and other Southeast Asian artists. The [Singapore Art Museum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Singapore_Art_Museum) focuses on contemporary art from a Southeast Asian perspective.[^cite_note-497] The [Red Dot](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Red_Dot_Design_Award) Design Museum celebrates exceptional art and design of objects for everyday life, hosting more than 1,000 items from 50 countries. The lotus-shaped [ArtScience Museum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ArtScience_Museum) hosts touring exhibitions that combine art with the sciences. Other major museums include the [Asian Civilisations Museum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Asian_Civilisations_Museum), the [Peranakan Museum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peranakan_Museum), and [The Arts House](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Arts_House).[^cite_note-498] [The Esplanade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Esplanade_%E2%80%93_Theatres_on_the_Bay) is Singapore's largest performing arts centre. In 2016 alone, it was the site of 5,900 free art and culture events.[^cite_note-499][^cite_note-CNA1-500]

Literature of Singapore, or "SingLit", consists of a collection of literary works by Singaporeans written chiefly in the country's four official languages: English, Malay, Mandarin, and Tamil. Singapore is increasingly regarded as having four sub-literatures instead of one. Many significant works have been translated and showcased in publications such as the literary journal *Singa*, published in the 1980s and 1990s with editors including [Edwin Thumboo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Edwin_Thumboo) and [Koh Buck Song](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Koh_Buck_Song), as well as in multilingual anthologies such as *Rhythms: A Singaporean Millennial Anthology Of Poetry* (2000), in which the poems were all translated three times each. A number of Singaporean writers such as [Tan Swie Hian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tan_Swie_Hian) and [Kuo Pao Kun](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kuo_Pao_Kun) have contributed work in more than one language.[^cite_note-501][^cite_note-502]

Singapore has a diverse music culture that ranges from pop and rock, to folk and classical. Western classical music plays a significant role in the cultural life in Singapore, with the [Singapore Symphony Orchestra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Singapore_Symphony_Orchestra) (SSO) instituted in 1979. Other notable western orchestras in Singapore include [Singapore National Youth Orchestra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Singapore_National_Youth_Orchestra)[^cite_note-503] and the community-based [Braddell Heights Symphony Orchestra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Braddell_Heights_Symphony_Orchestra).[^cite_note-504] Many orchestras and ensembles are also found in secondary schools and junior colleges. Various communities have their own distinct ethnic musical traditions: Chinese, Malays, Indians, and Eurasians. With their traditional forms of music and various modern musical styles, the fusion of different forms account for the musical diversity in the country.[^cite_note-garland-505] The nation's lively urban musical scene has made it a centre for international performances and festivals in the region. Some of Singapore's best known pop singers include [Stefanie Sun](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stefanie_Sun), [JJ Lin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/JJ_Lin), [Liang Wern Fook](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liang_Wern_Fook), [Taufik Batisah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taufik_Batisah) and [Dick Lee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dick_Lee), who is famous for composing [National Day](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Day_\(Singapore\)) theme songs, including *Home*.[^cite_note-506][^cite_note-507]

### Media

Main article: [Media of Singapore](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Media_of_Singapore)

See also: [Telecommunications in Singapore](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Telecommunications_in_Singapore)



The [Ministry of Communications and Information](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ministry_of_Communications_and_Information) oversees the development of [infocomm](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Infocomm), media and [the arts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_arts).

Companies linked to the government control much of the domestic media in Singapore.[^cite_note-freedomhouse_a-508] [MediaCorp](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/MediaCorp) operates most [free-to-air television channels](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Television_in_Singapore) and [free-to-air radio stations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_radio_stations_in_Singapore) in Singapore. There are a total of six free-to-air TV channels offered by MediaCorp.[^cite_note-509] [StarHub TV](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/StarHub_TV) and [Singtel TV](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Singtel_TV) also offer [IPTV](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/IPTV) with channels from all around the world.[^cite_note-510][^cite_note-511] [SPH Media Trust](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SPH_Media_Trust), a body with close links to the government, controls most of the newspaper industry in Singapore.[^cite_note-BBC_Media-512]

Singapore's media industry has sometimes been criticised for being overly regulated and lacking in freedom by human rights groups such as [Freedom House](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Freedom_House).[^cite_note-freedomhouse_a-508] Self-censorship among journalists is said to be common.[^cite_note-BBC_Media-512] In 2023, Singapore was ranked 129 on the Press Freedom Index published by [Reporters Without Borders](526054.md), up from 139 the previous year.[^cite_note-513] The [Media Development Authority](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Media_Development_Authority) regulates Singaporean media, claiming to balance the demand for choice and protection against offensive and harmful material.[^cite_note-Mica-514] Private ownership of TV satellite dishes is banned.[^cite_note-BBC_Media-512]

[Internet in Singapore](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Internet_in_Singapore) is provided by state-owned [Singtel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Singtel), partially state-owned [Starhub](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/StarHub) and [M1 Limited](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M1_Limited) as well as some other business [internet service providers](100245.md) (ISPs) that offer residential service plans of speeds up to 2 [Gbit/s](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gbit/s) as of spring 2015.[^cite_note-515] [Equinix](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Equinix) (332 participants) and the [Singapore Internet Exchange](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Singapore_Internet_Exchange) (70 participants) are [Internet exchange points](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Internet_exchange_point) where Internet service providers and [Content delivery networks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Content_delivery_network) exchange Internet traffic between their networks ([autonomous systems](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Autonomous_system_\(Internet\))) in various locations in Singapore.[^cite_note-516][^cite_note-517] In the mid-1980s to 1990s, Singaporeans could also use the locally based videotext service [Singapore Teleview](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Singapore_Teleview) to communicate with one another.[^cite_note-sandfort-518] The phrase *Intelligent Island* arose in the 1990s in reference to the island nation's early adaptive relationship with the internet.[^cite_note-sandfort-518][^cite_note-519]

In 2016, there were an estimated 4.7 million internet users in Singapore, representing 82.5% of the population.[^cite_note-520] The Singapore government does not engage in widespread censoring of the internet,[^cite_note-521] but it maintains a list of one hundred websites—mostly pornographic—that it blocks from home internet access as a "symbolic statement of the Singaporean community's stand on harmful and undesirable content on the Internet".[^cite_note-522][^cite_note-523] Singapore has the world's highest [smartphone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Smartphone) penetration rates, in surveys by [Deloitte](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deloitte)[^cite_note-524][^cite_note-525] and the Google Consumer Barometer—at 89% and 85% of the population respectively in 2014.[^cite_note-526] The overall mobile phone penetration rate is at 148 mobile phone subscribers per 100 people.[^cite_note-singstat2014mobile-527]

### Cuisine

Main article: [Singaporean cuisine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Singaporean_cuisine)

Satay stalls along Boon Tat Street next to Telok Ayer Market, better known as Lau Pa Sat

[Lau Pa Sat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lau_Pa_Sat) hawker centre in the financial district. [Satay](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Satay) cart-stalls roll in after dusk, on a side street.

Singapore's diversity of cuisine is touted as a reason to visit the country, due to its combination of convenience, variety, quality, and price.[^cite_note-Foodways-528] Local food items generally relate to a particular ethnicity – Chinese, Malay and Indian; but the diversity of cuisine has increased further by the hybridisation of different styles (e.g., the [Peranakan cuisine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peranakan_cuisine), a mix of Chinese and Malay cuisine). In hawker centres, cultural diffusion is exemplified by traditionally Malay hawker stalls also selling Tamil food. [Hainanese chicken rice](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hainanese_chicken_rice), based on the Hainanese dish [Wenchang chicken](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wenchang_chicken), is considered Singapore's national dish.[^cite_note-farleyBBC6nov2015-529][^cite_note-lingCNN14apr2010-530]

The city-state has a burgeoning food scene ranging from hawker centres (open-air), food courts (air-conditioned), coffee shops (open-air with up to a dozen hawker stalls), cafes, fast food, simple kitchens, casual, celebrity and high-end restaurants.[^cite_note-streetfood-531] [Cloud kitchens](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cloud_kitchen) and food delivery are also on the rise, with 70% of residents ordering from delivery apps at least once a month.[^cite_note-532][^cite_note-533] Many international [celebrity chef](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Celebrity_chef) restaurants are located within the [integrated resorts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Integrated_resort).[^cite_note-534] Religious dietary strictures exist (Muslims do not eat pork and Hindus do not eat beef), and there is also a significant group of vegetarians. The [Singapore Food Festival](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Singapore_Food_Festival) which celebrates Singapore's cuisine is held annually in July.[^cite_note-SFF-535]

Prior to the 1980s, [street food](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Street_food) was sold mainly by immigrants from China, India, and Malaysia to other immigrants seeking a familiar taste. In Singapore, street food has long been associated with [hawker centres](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hawker_centre) with communal seating areas. Typically, these centres have a few dozen to hundreds of food stalls, with each specialising in one or more related dishes.[^cite_note-fodor-536][^cite_note-streetfood-531] While street food can be found in many countries, the variety and reach of centralised hawker centres that serve heritage street food in Singapore is unique.[^cite_note-Hawker2007-537] In 2018, there were 114 hawker centres spread across the city centre and heartland housing estates. They are maintained by the [National Environment Agency](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Environment_Agency), which also grades each food stall for hygiene. The largest hawker centre is located on the second floor of Chinatown Complex, and contains over 200 stalls.[^cite_note-Hawker2007-537] The complex is also home to the cheapest Michelin-starred meal in the world – a plate of [soya-sauce](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soy_sauce) chicken rice or noodles for S\$2 (US\$1.50). Two street food stalls in the city are the first in the world to be awarded a Michelin star, obtaining a single star each.[^cite_note-538]

### Sport and recreation

Main article: [Sport in Singapore](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sport_in_Singapore)



[Joseph Schooling](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joseph_Schooling) is a gold medalist and Olympic record holder at the [Rio 2016 Games – 100 m butterfly](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Swimming_at_the_2016_Summer_Olympics_%E2%80%93_Men's_100_metre_butterfly).

[^cite_note-Schooling-Rio-539]

In 1948, [Lloyd Valberg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lloyd_Valberg) participated in the [1948 Summer Olympics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1948_Summer_Olympics) in London, which made him the first Singaporean to participate in the [Olympic Games](22576.md). The development of private sports and recreation clubs began in the 19th century colonial Singapore, with clubs founded during this time including the Cricket Club, the [Singapore Recreation Club](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Singapore_Recreation_Club), the Singapore Swimming Club, and the Hollandse Club.[^cite_note-540] Weightlifter [Tan Howe Liang](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tan_Howe_Liang) was Singapore's first Olympic medalist, winning a silver at the [1960 Rome Games](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1960_Summer_Olympics).[^cite_note-541] Singapore hosted the inaugural [2010 Summer Youth Olympics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2010_Summer_Youth_Olympics), in which 3,600 athletes from 204 nations competed in 26 sports.[^cite_note-Singapore_to_host_first_edition_of_the_Youth_Olympic_Games_in_2010-542]

Indoor and water sports are some of the most popular sports in Singapore. At the [2016 Rio Olympics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2016_Rio_Olympics), [Joseph Schooling](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joseph_Schooling) won Singapore's first Olympic gold medal, claiming the [100-metre butterfly](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Swimming_at_the_2016_Summer_Olympics_%E2%80%93_Men's_100_metre_butterfly) in a new Olympic record time of 50.39 seconds.[^cite_note-Schooling-Rio-539] Singapore sailors have had success on the international stage, with their [Optimist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Optimist_\(dinghy\)) team being considered among the best in the world.[^cite_note-543][^cite_note-544] Despite its size, the country has dominated swim meets in the [Southeast Asia Games](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southeast_Asia_Games). Its men's water polo team won the SEA Games gold medal for the 27th time in 2017, continuing Singapore sport's longest winning streak.[^cite_note-545] At the [2024 Paris Olympics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2024_Paris_Olympics), [Max Maeder](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Max_Maeder) won Singapore's first Olympic medal in [sailing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sailing_\(sport\)), achieving bronze at the [Men's Formula Kite](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sailing_at_the_2024_Summer_Olympics_%E2%80%93_Men's_Formula_Kite) on [National Day](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Day_\(Singapore\)). At 17, he was also Singapore's youngest Olympic medalist.[^cite_note-546]

Singapore's women's table tennis team were silver medalists at the [2008 Beijing Olympics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2008_Beijing_Olympics).[^cite_note-547][^cite_note-548] They became world champions in 2010 when they beat China at the [World Team Table Tennis Championships](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_Team_Table_Tennis_Championships) in Russia, breaking China's 19-year winning streak.[^cite_note-549] In 2021, Singapore's [Loh Kean Yew](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Loh_Kean_Yew) achieved a "World Champion" status when he won a badminton gold at the [2021 BWF World Championships](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2021_BWF_World_Championships) [men's singles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2021_BWF_World_Championships_%E2%80%93_Men's_singles), which is one of the most prestigious badminton tournaments alongside the [Summer Olympics badminton tournaments](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Badminton_at_the_Summer_Olympics).[^cite_note-550]

Singapore's [football](10568.md) league, the [Singapore Premier League](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Singapore_Premier_League), was launched in 1996 as the S.League and comprises eight clubs, including one foreign team.[^cite_note-551][^cite_note-552] The [Singapore Slingers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Singapore_Slingers) is one of the inaugural teams in the [ASEAN Basketball League](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ASEAN_Basketball_League), which was founded in October 2009.[^cite_note-553] [Kranji Racecourse](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kranji_Racecourse) is run by the [Singapore Turf Club](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Singapore_Turf_Club) and hosts several meetings per week, including international races—notably the [Singapore Airlines International Cup](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Singapore_Airlines_International_Cup).[^cite_note-554]

Singapore began hosting a round of the [Formula One World Championship](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Formula_One_World_Championship), the [Singapore Grand Prix](9305401.md) at the [Marina Bay Street Circuit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marina_Bay_Street_Circuit) in 2008. It was the inaugural F1 night race,[^cite_note-News_–_Singapore_confirms_2008_night_race-555] and the first F1 street race in Asia.[^cite_note-1stStreet-556] It is considered a signature event on the F1 calendar.[^cite_note-557] [ONE Championship](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ONE_Championship) was founded in Singapore, a major [Mixed Martial Arts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mixed_Martial_Arts) (MMA) promotion in Asia.[^cite_note-558]

[^cite_note-2]: &#x20;Singapore has no official distinct capital city as it is a [city-state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/City-state). Prior to its independence as a [sovereign state](23604120.md) in 1965, the [City of Singapore](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/City_of_Singapore_\(historical_entity\)) served as the capital. Today, the [Central Area](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Central_Area,_Singapore) occupies this role in practice, functioning as a *de facto* capital without any formal designation.<sup>[\[1\]](#cite_note-City-state-1)</sup>

[^cite_note-5]: &#x20;In Singapore, proportions of ethnic groups publicly released are based only on the resident population, which comprises Singaporean citizens (SC) and permanent residents (PR).<sup>[\[3\]](#cite_note-census2020-4)</sup>

[^cite_note-7]: &#x20;In Singapore, proportions of religious denominations publicly released are based only on the resident population, which comprises Singaporean citizens (SC) and permanent residents (PR).<sup>[\[4\]](#cite_note-census2025-6)</sup>

[^cite_note-138]: &#x20;Some of Singapore's islands use the English term "island" (e.g. Jurong Island or Sentosa Island), while others retain the Malay term "pulau" in their common names (e.g. Pulau Ubin or Pulau Tekong), largely for historical or cultural reasons. In Singapore English, "island" and "pulau" are often used interchangeably, with "pulau" functioning as a synonym for the English word in this specific context.
